Prioritise the Best Variation Thanks: Supported by the EU HORIZON 2020 MSCA RISE project 778233 “Behavioural Application Program Interfaces” (BehAPI)
Wen Kokke ORCID:0000-0002-1662-0381, Ornela Dardha ORCID:0000-0001-9927-7875

Abstract
. Binary session types guarantee communication safety and session fidelity, but alone they cannot rule out deadlocks arising from the interleaving of different sessions. In Classical Processes (CP) [ Wad14 ] —a process calculus based on classical linear logic—deadlock freedom is guaranteed by combining channel creation and parallel composition under the same logical cut rule. Similarly, in Good Variation (GV) [ Wad15 , LM15 ] —a linear concurrent λ \lambda -calculus—deadlock freedom is guaranteed by combining channel creation and thread spawning under the same operation, called fork. In both CP and GV, deadlock freedom is achieved at the expense of expressivity, as the only processes allowed are tree-structured. This is true more broadly than for CP and GV, and it holds for all works in the research line based on Curry-Howard correspondences between linear logic and session types, starting with Caires and Pfenning [ CP10 ] . To overcome the limitations of tree-structured processes, Dardha and Gay [ DG18a ] define Priority CP (PCP), which allows cyclic-structured processes and restores deadlock freedom by using priorities , in line with Kobayashi and Padovani [ Kob06 , Pad14 ] . Following PCP, we present Priority GV (PGV), a variant of GV which decouples channel creation from thread spawning. Consequently, we type cyclic-structured processes and restore deadlock freedom by using priorities. We show that our type system is sound by proving subject reduction and progress. We define an encoding from PCP to PGV and prove that the encoding preserves typing and is sound and complete with respect to the operational semantics.



Dec. 18, 2021Dec. 18, 2023

†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 19, Issue 4, Paper 28

1.
Introduction

Session types
[
Hon93
,
THK94
,
HVK98
]
are a type formalism that ensures
communication channels
are used according to their protocols, much like,
e
.
g
., data types ensure that functions are used according to their signature. Session types have been studied in many settings. Most notably, they have been defined for the
\pi
-calculus
[
Hon93
,
THK94
,
HVK98
]
, a foundational calculus for communication and concurrency, and the concurrent
\lambda
-calculi
[
GV10
]
, including the main focus of our paper: Good Variation
[
Wad15
,
LM15
, GV]
.

GV is a concurrent
\lambda
-calculus with
binary
session types, where each channel is shared between exactly two processes. Binary session types guarantee two crucial properties:
communication safety
—
e
.
g
., if the protocol says to transmit an integer, you transmit an integer—and
session fidelity
—
e
.
g
., if the protocol says send, you send. A third crucial property is
deadlock freedom
, which ensures that processes do not have cyclic dependencies—
e
.
g
., when two processes wait for each other to send a value. Binary session types alone are insufficient to rule out deadlocks arising from interleaved sessions, but several additional techniques have been developed to guarantee deadlock freedom in session-typed
\pi
-calculus and concurrent
\lambda
-calculus.

In the
\pi
-calculus literature, there have been several developments of Curry-Howard correspondences between session-typed
\pi
-calculus and linear logic
[
Gir87
]
: Caires and Pfenning’s
\pi
DILL
[
CP10
]
corresponds to dual intuitionistic linear logic
[
Bar96
]
, and Wadler’s Classical Processes
[
Wad14
, CP]
corresponds to classical linear logic
[
Gir87
, CLL]
. Both calculi guarantee deadlock freedom, which they achieve by the combination of binary session types with a restriction on the way processes can be connected by channels: two processes can share at most one channel, and, more generally, information transmitted between any two processes must pass through one unique series of channels and intermediate processes. We refer to such processes as
tree-structured
, because the
communication graph
—where the vertices are ready processes and two vertices are connected by an edge if and only if the corresponding processes share a channel—is a tree. For
\pi
DILL, CP, and GV, tree-structure follows from a syntactic restriction: the combination of name restriction and parallel composition into a single syntactic construct, corresponding to the logical cut.

There are many downsides to combining name restriction and parallel composition, such as lack of modularity, difficulty typing structural congruence and formulating label-transition semantics. GV, specifically, struggles with a complicated metatheory due to the mismatch between its term language—where restriction and parallel composition are combined—and its configuration language—where they are not. There have been various approaches to decoupling restriction and parallel composition.
Hypersequent CP
[
MP18
,
KMP19a
,
KMP19b
, HCP]
, Hypersequent GV
[
FKD+21
]
, and Linear Compositional Choreographies
[
CMS18
]
decouple them, but maintain the tree-structure of processes and a correspondence to linear logic,
e
.
g
., while the typing rules for HCP are no longer exactly the proof rules for CLL, every typing derivations in HCP is isomorphic a proof in CLL, and vice versa.
Priority CP
[
DG18b
, PCP]
weakens the correspondence to CLL. PCP is a non-conservative extension of CLL: every proof in CLL can be translated to a typing derivation in PCP
[
DP22
]
, but PCP can prove strictly more theorems, including (partial) bijections between several CLL connectives. In exchange, PCP has a much more expressive language which allows cyclic-structured processes. PCP decouples CP’s cut rule into two separate constructs: one for parallel composition via a mix rule, and one for name restriction via a cycle rule. To restore deadlock freedom, PCP uses
priorities

[
Kob06
,
Pad14
]
. Priorities encode the
order of actions
and rule out bad cyclic interleavings. Dardha and Gay
[
DG18b
]
prove cycle-elimination for PCP, adapting the cut-elimination proof for classical linear logic, and deadlock freedom follows as a corollary.

CP and GV are related via a pair of translations which satisfy simulation
[
LM16
]
, and which can be tweaked to satisfy operational correspondence. The two calculi share the same strong guarantees. GV achieves deadlock freedom via a similar syntactic restriction: it combines channel creation and thread spawning into a single operation, called “fork”, which is related to the cut construct in CP. Unfortunately, as with CP, this syntactic restriction has its downsides.

Our aim is to develop a more expressive version of GV while maintaining deadlock freedom. While process calculi have their advantages,
e
.
g
., their succinctness compared to concurrent
\lambda
-calculi, we chose to work with GV for several reasons. In general, concurrent
\lambda
-calculi support higher-order functions, and have a capability for abstraction not usually present in process calculi. Within a concurrent
\lambda
-calculus, one can derive extensions of the communication capabilities of the language via well-understood extensions of the functional fragment,
e
.
g
., we can derive internal/external choice from sum types. Concurrent
\lambda
-calculi, among other languages, maintain a clear separation between the program which the user writes and the configurations which represent the state of the system as it evaluates the program. However, our main motivation is that results obtained for
\lambda
-calculi transfer more easily to real-world functional programming languages. Case in point: we easily adapted the type system of PGV to Linear Haskell
[
BBN+18
]
, which gives us a library for deadlock-free session-typed programming
[
KD21a
]
.
The benefit of working specifically with GV, as opposed to other concurrent
\lambda
-calculi, is its relation to CP
[
Wad14
]
, and its formal properties, including deadlock freedom.

We thus pose our research question for GV:

RQ:

Can we design a more expressive GV which guarantees deadlock freedom for cyclic-structured processes?

We follow the line of work from CP to Priority CP, and present Priority GV (PGV), a variant of GV which decouples channel creation from thread spawning, thus allowing cyclic-structured processes, but which nonetheless guarantees deadlock freedom via priorities. This closes the circle of the connection between CP and GV
[
Wad14
]
, and their priority-based versions, PCP
[
DG18b
]
and PGV.
We cannot straightforwardly adapt the priority typing from PCP to PGV, as PGV adds higher-order functions. Instead, the priority typing for PGV follow the work by Padovani and Novara
[
PN15
]
.

We make the following main contributions:

(1)

Priority GV
. We present Priority GV (
Section

2
, PGV), a session-typed functional language with priorities, and prove
subject reduction
(
3.5
) and
progress
(
3.13
).

We addresses several problems in the original GV language, most notably:

(a)

PGV does not require the pseudo-type
S^{\sharp}
;

(b)

Structural congruence is type preserving.

PGV answers our research question positively as it allows cyclic-structured binary session-typed processes that are deadlock free.

(2)

Translation from PCP to PGV
.
We present a
sound and complete encoding
of PCP
[
DG18b
]
in PGV (
Section

4
). We prove the encoding preserves typing (
4.6
) and satisfies operational correspondence (
4.7
and
4.10
).

To obtain a tight correspondence, we update PCP, moving away from commuting conversions and reduction as cut elimination towards reduction based on structural congruence, as it is standard in process calculi.

This paper is an improved and extended version of a paper published at FORTE 2021 international conference
[
KD21b
]
. We present detailed examples and complete proofs of our technical results.

2.
Priority GV

We present Priority GV (PGV), a session-typed functional language based on GV
[
Wad15
,
LM15
]
which uses priorities à la Kobayashi and Padovani
[
Kob06
,
PN15
]
to enforce deadlock freedom.
Priority GV is more flexible than GV because it allows processes to share more than one communication channel.

We illustrate this with two programs in PGV,
2.1
and
2.2
. Each program contains two processes—the main process, and the child process created by
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}}
—which communicate using
two
channels. The child process receives a unit over the channel
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}/{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}
, and then sends a unit over the channel
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}/{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
. The main process does one of two things:

(a)

in
2.1
, it sends a unit over the channel
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}/{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}
, and then waits to receive a unit over the channel
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}/{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
;

(b)

in
2.2
, it does these in the opposite order, which results in a deadlock.

PGV is more expressive than GV:
2.1
is typeable and guaranteed to be deadlock-free in PGV, but is not typeable in GV
[
Wad14
]
and not guaranteed deadlock-free in GV’s predecessor
[
GV10
]
. We believe PGV is a non-conservative
extension
of GV, as CP can be embedded in a Kobayashi-style system
[
DP18
]
.

Example 2.1
.

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{let}\;(x,x^{\prime})\mathbin{=}\mathbf{new}\;()\;\mathbf{in}\\
\mathbf{let}\;(y,y^{\prime})\mathbin{=}\mathbf{new}\;()\;\mathbf{in}\\
\mathbf{spawn}\left(\lambda().\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathbf{let}\;((),x^{\prime})\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;{x^{\prime}}\;\mathbf{in}\;}\\
{\mathbf{let}\;y\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;{((),y)}\;\mathbf{in}\;}\\
{\mathbf{wait}\;{x^{\prime}};\mathbf{close}\;{y}}\end{array}\right);\\
\underline{\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;{((),x)}\;\mathbf{in}\;}\\
\underline{\mathbf{let}\;((),y^{\prime})\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;{y^{\prime}}\;\mathbf{in}\;}\\
{\mathbf{close}\;{x};\mathbf{wait}\;{y^{\prime}}}\end{array}}

Example 2.2
.

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{let}\;(x,x^{\prime})\mathbin{=}\mathbf{new}\;()\;\mathbf{in}\\
\mathbf{let}\;(y,y^{\prime})\mathbin{=}\mathbf{new}\;()\;\mathbf{in}\\
\mathbf{spawn}\left(\lambda().\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathbf{let}\;((),x^{\prime})\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;{x^{\prime}}\;\mathbf{in}\;}\\
{\mathbf{let}\;y\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;{((),y)}\;\mathbf{in}\;}\\
{\mathbf{wait}\;{x^{\prime}};\mathbf{close}\;{y}}\end{array}\right);\\
\underline{\mathbf{let}\;((),y^{\prime})\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;{y^{\prime}}\;\mathbf{in}\;}\\
\underline{\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;{((),x)}\;\mathbf{in}\;}\\
{\mathbf{close}\;{x};\mathbf{wait}\;{y^{\prime}}}\end{array}}

2.1.
Syntax of Types and Terms

Session types

Session types (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}
) are defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\end{array}

Session types
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}
describe the endpoints of a channel over which we send or receive a value of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
, and then proceed as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}
. Types
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}
describe endpoints of a channel whose communication has finished, and over which we must synchronise before closing the channel. Each connective in a session type is annotated with a
priority

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}\in\mathbb{N}
.

Types

Types (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}
) are defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\times}{U}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{U}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{U}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\end{array}

Types
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\times}{U}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{U}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}}
are the standard linear
\lambda
-calculus product type, unit type, sum type, and empty type.
Type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{U}}
is the standard linear function type, annotated with
priority bounds

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}}\}
.
Every session type is also a type.
Given a function with type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{U}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}
is a
lower bound
on the priorities of the endpoints captured by the body of the function, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}
is an
upper bound
on the priority of the communications that take place as a result of applying the function. The type of pure functions
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap}{U}}
,
i
.
e
., those which perform no communications, is syntactic sugar for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{U}}
. The lower bound for a pure function is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}}
as pure functions never start communicating. For similar reasons, the upper bound for a pure function is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}
.

We postulate that the only function types—and, consequently, sequents—that are inhabited in PGV are pure functions and functions
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{U}}
for which
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}<{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}
.

Typing Environments

Typing environments
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Theta}
associate types to names. Environments are linear, so two environments can only be combined as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}
if their names are distinct,
i
.
e
.,
\fv({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})\cap\fv({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta})=\varnothing
.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\varnothing}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\end{array}

Type Duality

Duality plays a crucial role in session types. The two endpoints of a channel are assigned dual types, ensuring that, for instance, whenever one program sends a value on a channel, the program on the other end is waiting to receive. Each session type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}
has a dual, written
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}
. Duality is an involutive function which preserves priorities:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{\overline{S}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{\overline{S}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{\mathbf{end}_{!}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{\mathbf{end}_{?}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}

Priorities

Function
\pr(\cdot)
returns the smallest priority of a session type. The type system guarantees that the top-most connective always holds the smallest priority, so we simply return the priority of the top-most connective:

\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}\qquad\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}\qquad\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}\qquad\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}

We extend the function
\pr(\cdot)
to types and typing contexts by returning the smallest priority in the type or context, or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top}
if there is no priority. We use
\sqcap
and
\sqcup
to denote the minimum and maximum, respectively:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\times}{U}})&=&\pr({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}})\sqcap\pr({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}})\\
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{U}})&=&\pr({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}})\sqcap\pr({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}})\\
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{U}})&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}\\
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A})&=&\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})\sqcap\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A})\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{lcl}\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}})&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}\\
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}})&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}\\
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S})&=&\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S})\\
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\varnothing})&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}\end{array}

Terms

Terms (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
) are defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda x.M}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M\;N}\\
&\;\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}()}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M;N}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(M,N)}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)\mathbin{=}M\;\mathbf{in}\;N}\\
&\;\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inl}\;M}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inr}\;M}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{case}\;L\;\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{absurd}\;M}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{new}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{send}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{recv}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{close}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{wait}}\end{array}

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}w}
range over variable names. Occasionally, we use
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}a}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}b}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}c}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}d}
. The term language is the standard linear
\lambda
-calculus with products, sums, and their units, extended with constants
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}
for the communication primitives.

Constants are best understood in conjunction with their typing and reduction rules in
Figs.

2
and
1
.

Briefly,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
links two endpoints together, forwarding messages from one to the other,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{new}}
creates a new channel and returns a pair of its endpoints, and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}}
spawns off its argument as a new thread.

The
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{send}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{recv}}
functions send and receive values on a channel.
However, since the typing rules for PGV ensure the linear usage of endpoints, they also return a new copy of the endpoint to continue the session.

The
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{close}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{wait}}
functions close a channel.

We use syntactic sugar to make terms more readable: we write
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}M\;\mathbf{in}\;N}
in place of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\lambda x.N)\;M}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda().M}
in place of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda z.z;M}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda(x,y).M}
in place of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda z.\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)\mathbin{=}z\;\mathbf{in}\;M}
.
We can recover GV’s
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}}
as
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda x.\mathbf{let}\;(y,z)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{new}\;()\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{spawn}\;{(\lambda().x\;y)};z}
.

Internal and External Choice

Typically, session-typed languages feature constructs for internal and external choice. In GV, these can be defined in terms of the core language, by sending or receiving a value of a sum type
[
LM15
]
. We use the following syntactic sugar for internal (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{S}\mathbin{\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{S^{\prime}}}
) and external (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{S}\mathbin{\with^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{S^{\prime}}}
) choice and their units:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{S}\mathbin{\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{S^{\prime}}}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{({\overline{S}}\mathbin{+}{\overline{S^{\prime}}})}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o+1}}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{S}\mathbin{\with^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{S^{\prime}}}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{({S}\mathbin{+}{S^{\prime}})}.{\mathbf{end}_{?}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o+1}}}}\\
\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\oplus}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}\{\}}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{\mathbf{0}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o+1}}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\with}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}\{\}}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{\mathbf{0}}.{\mathbf{end}_{?}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o+1}}}}\\
\end{array}

As the syntax for units suggests, these are the binary and nullary forms of the more common n-ary choice constructs
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\oplus}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in{I}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\with}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in{I}}}
, which one may obtain generalising the sum types to variant types. For simplicity, we present only the binary and nullary forms.

Similarly, we use syntactic sugar for the term forms of choice, which combine sending and receiving with the introduction and elimination forms for the sum and empty types. There are two constructs for binary internal choice, expressed using the meta-variable
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}
which ranges over
\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inl}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inr}}\}
. As there is no introduction for the empty type, there is no construct for nullary internal choice:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{select}\;\ell}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda x.\mathbf{let}\;(y,z)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{new}\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{close}\;(\mathbf{send}\;{(\ell\;y,x)});z}\\
\lx@intercol{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{offer}\;L\;\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\}}\triangleq\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\lx@intercol\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(z,w)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;{L}\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{wait}\;{w};\mathbf{case}\;z\;\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{offer}\;L\;\{\}}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(z,w)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;L\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{wait}\;{w};\mathbf{absurd}\;z}\end{array}

2.2.
Operational Semantics

Configurations

Priority GV terms are evaluated as part of a configuration of processes. Configurations are defined by the following grammar:

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ}\hfill\qquad\hfill{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{E}}\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi\;M}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}\parallel\conf{D}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})\conf{C}}

Configurations (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{E}}
) consist of threads
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi\;M}
, parallel compositions
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}\parallel\conf{D}}
, and name restrictions
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})\conf{C}}
. To preserve the functional nature of PGV, where programs return a single value, we use flags (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}
) to differentiate between the main thread, marked
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}
, and child threads created by
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}}
, marked
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ}
. Only the main thread returns a value. We determine the flag of a configuration by combining the flags of all threads in that configuration:

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}+{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}\hfill\qquad\hfill{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ}+{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}\hfill\qquad\hfill{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ}+{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ}\hfill\qquad\hfill({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}+{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}\;\text{is undefined})\hfill\qquad\hfill

To distinguish between the use of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ}
to mark child threads
[
LM15
]
and the use of the meta-variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
for priorities
[
DG18b
]
, they are typeset in a different font and colour.

Values

Values (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}W}
), evaluation contexts (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E}
), thread evaluation contexts (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}}
), and configuration contexts (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{G}}
) are defined by the following grammars:

\begin{array}[t]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}W}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda x.M}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}()}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(V,W)}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inl}\;V}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inr}\;V}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\square}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E\;M}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V\;E}\\
&\;\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E;N}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(E,M)}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(V,E)}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)\mathbin{=}E\;\mathbf{in}\;M}\\
&\;\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inl}\;E}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inr}\;E}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{case}\;E\;\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{absurd}\;E}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi\;E}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{G}}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\square}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{G}\parallel\conf{C}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)\conf{G}}\end{array}

Values are the subset of terms which cannot reduce further.
Evaluation contexts are one-hole term contexts,
i
.
e
., terms with exactly one hole, written
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\square}
. We write
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E[M]}
for the evaluation context
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E}
with its hole replaced by the term
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
. Evaluation contexts are specifically those one-hole term contexts under which term reduction can take place.
Thread contexts are a convenient way to lift the notion of evaluation contexts to threads. We write
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}[M]}
for the thread context
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}}
with its hole replaced by the term
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
.
Configuration contexts are one-hole configuration contexts,
i
.
e
., configurations with exactly one hole, written
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\square}
. Specifically, configuration contexts are those one-hole term contexts under which configuration reduction can take place. The definition for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{G}}
only gives the case in which the hole is in the left-most parallel process,
i
.
e
., it only defines
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{G}\parallel\conf{C}}
and not
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}\parallel\conf{G}}
. The latter is not needed, as
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\parallel}
is symmetric under structural congruence, though it would be harmless to add. We write
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{G}[\conf{C}]}
for the evaluation context
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{G}}
with its hole replaced by the term
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
.

Reduction Relation

Term reduction.

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\LabTirName{E-Lam}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\lambda x.M)\;V}&\longrightarrow_{M}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M\{V/x\}}\\
\LabTirName{E-Unit}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}();M}&\longrightarrow_{M}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}\\
\LabTirName{E-Pair}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)\mathbin{=}(V,W)\;\mathbf{in}\;M}&\longrightarrow_{M}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M\{V/x\}\{W/y\}}\\
\LabTirName{E-Inl}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{case}\;\mathbf{inl}\;V\;\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}}&\longrightarrow_{M}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M\{V/x\}}\\
\LabTirName{E-Inr}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{case}\;\mathbf{inr}\;V\;\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}}&\longrightarrow_{M}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N\{V/y\}}\end{array}\\
\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}\longrightarrow_{M}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E[M]}\longrightarrow_{M}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E[M^{\prime}]}}\quad\textsc{lab=E-Lift}

Structural congruence.

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\LabTirName{SC-LinkSwap}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}[\mathbf{link}\;{(x,y)}]}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}[\mathbf{link}\;{(y,x)}]}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ResLink}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\phi\;\mathbf{link}\;(x,y))}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi\;()}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ResSwap}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)\conf{C}}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu yx)\conf{C}}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ResComm}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\nu zw)\conf{C}}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu zw)(\nu xy)\conf{C}},\text{ if }{\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}\}\cap\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}w}\}=\varnothing}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ResExt}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\conf{C}\parallel\conf{D})}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}\parallel(\nu xy)\conf{D}},\text{ if }{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}\notin\fv({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}})}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ParNil}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}\parallel\circ{()}}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ParComm}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}\parallel\conf{D}}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D}\parallel\conf{C}}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ParAssoc}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}\parallel(\conf{D}\parallel\conf{E})}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\conf{C}\parallel\conf{D})\parallel\conf{E}}\end{array}

Configuration reduction.

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{llcl}\LabTirName{E-Link}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\conf{F}[\mathbf{link}\;(w,x)]\parallel\conf{C})}&\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}[()]\parallel\conf{C}\{w/y\}}\\
\LabTirName{E-New}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}[\mathbf{new}\;()]}&\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\conf{F}[(x,y)])},\text{ if }{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}\notin\fv({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}})\\
\LabTirName{E-Spawn}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}[(\mathbf{spawn}\;V)]}&\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}[()]\parallel\circ\;V\;()}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{llcl}\LabTirName{E-Send}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\conf{F}[\mathbf{send}\;{(V,x)}]\parallel\conf{F^{\prime}}[\mathbf{recv}\;{y}])}&\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\conf{F}[x]\parallel\conf{F^{\prime}}[(V,y)])}\\
\LabTirName{E-Close}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\conf{F}[\mathbf{wait}\;{x}]\parallel\conf{F^{\prime}}[\mathbf{close}\;{y}])}&\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}[()]\parallel\conf{F^{\prime}}[()]}\end{array}\end{array}\\
\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C^{\prime}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{G}[\conf{C}]}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{G}[\conf{C^{\prime}}]}}\quad\textsc{lab=E-LiftC}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}\longrightarrow_{M}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}[M]}\longrightarrow_{M}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}[M^{\prime}]}}\quad\textsc{lab=E-LiftM}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C^{\prime}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C^{\prime}}}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D^{\prime}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D^{\prime}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D}}}\quad\textsc{lab=E-LiftSC}

Figure 1.
Operational Semantics for PGV.

We factor the reduction relation of PGV into a deterministic reduction on terms (
\longrightarrow_{M}
) and a non-deterministic reduction on configurations (
\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}
), see
Fig.

1
. We write
\longrightarrow_{M}^{+}
and
\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{+}
for the transitive closures, and
\longrightarrow_{M}^{\star}
and
\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{\star}
for the reflexive-transitive closures.

Term reduction is the standard call-by-value, left-to-right evaluation for GV, and only deviates from reduction for the linear
\lambda
-calculus in that it reduces terms to values or ready terms waiting to perform a communication action.

Configuration reduction resembles evaluation for a process calculus:
\LabTirName
E-Link,
\LabTirName
E-Send, and
\LabTirName
E-Close perform communications,
\LabTirName
E-LiftC allows reduction under configuration contexts, and
\LabTirName
E-LiftSC embeds a structural congruence
\equiv
. The remaining rules mediate between the process calculus and the functional language:
\LabTirName
E-New and
\LabTirName
E-Spawn evaluate the
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{new}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}}
constructs, creating the equivalent configuration constructs, and
\LabTirName
E-LiftM embeds term reduction.

Structural congruence satisfies the following axioms:
\LabTirName{SC-LinkSwap}
allows swapping channels in the link process.
\LabTirName{SC-ResLink}
allows restriction to be applied to link which is structurally equivalent to the terminated process, thus allowing elimination of unnecessary restrictions.
\LabTirName{SC-ResSwap}
allows swapping channels and
\LabTirName{SC-ResComm}
states that restriction is commutative.
\LabTirName{SC-ResExt}
is the standard scope extrusion rule. Rules
\LabTirName{SC-ParNil}
,
\LabTirName{SC-ParComm}
and
\LabTirName{SC-ParAssoc}
state that parallel composition uses the terminated process as the neutral element; it is commutative and associative.

While our configuration reduction is based on the standard evaluation for GV, the increased expressiveness of PGV allows us to simplify the relation on two counts.

(i)

We decompose the
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}}
construct
.
In GV,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}}
creates a new channel, spawns a child thread, and, when the child thread finishes, it closes the channel to its parent. In PGV, these are three separate operations:
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{new}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{close}}
. We no longer require that every child thread finishes by returning a terminated channel. Consequently, we also simplify the evaluation of the
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
construct.

Intuitively, evaluating
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
causes a substitution: if we have a channel bound as
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}\;(w,x)}
replaces all occurrences of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
by
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}w}
. However, in GV,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
is required to return a terminated channel, which means that the semantics for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}link}
must create a fresh channel of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}}/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}
. The endpoint of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}}
is returned by the
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}link}
construct, and a
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{wait}}
on the other endpoint guards the actual substitution. In PGV, evaluating
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
simply causes a substitution.

(ii)

Our structural congruence is type preserving
. Consequently, we can embed it directly into the reduction relation. In GV, this is not the case, and subject reduction relies on proving that if the result of rewriting via
\equiv
followed by reducing via
\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}
is an ill-typed configuration, we can rewrite it to a well-typed configuration via
\equiv
.

2.3.
Typing Rules

Static Typing Rules.

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Var}\par\frac{}{\varnothing\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Const}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda x.M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{U}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Lam}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q^{\prime}}}}{U}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta})\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}<{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q^{\prime}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M\;N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-App}\\
\frac{}{\varnothing\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}()}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Unit}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M;N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-LetUnit}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(M,N)}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\times}{U}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Pair}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\times}{T^{\prime}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T^{\prime}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T^{\prime}})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)\mathbin{=}M\;\mathbf{in}\;N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-LetPair}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\quad\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inl}\;M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{U}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Inl}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}\quad\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inr}\;M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{U}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Inr}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{T^{\prime}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T^{\prime}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{case}\;L\;\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-CaseSum}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{absurd}\;M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Absurd}

Type Schemas for Constants.

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{{S}\mathbin{\times}{\overline{S}}}\mathbin{\multimap}{\mathbf{1}}}\par{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{new}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\mathbf{1}}\mathbin{\multimap}{{S}\mathbin{\times}{\overline{S}}}}\par{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{({\mathbf{1}}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\mathbf{1}})}\mathbin{\multimap}{\mathbf{1}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{send}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{{T}\mathbin{\times}{!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{S}}\par{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{recv}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{{T}\mathbin{\times}{S}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{close}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\mathbf{end}_{!}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{\mathbf{1}}}\par{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{wait}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\mathbf{end}_{?}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{\mathbf{1}}}

Runtime Typing Rules.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet\;M}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Main}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;M}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Child}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)\conf{C}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Res}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi+\phi^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}\parallel\conf{D}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Par}

Figure 2.
Typing Rules for PGV.

Terms Typing

Typing rules for terms are at the top of
Fig.

2
.
Terms are typed by a judgement
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
stating that “a term
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
has type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
and an upper bound on its priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}
under the typing environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}
”. Typing for the linear
\lambda
-calculus is standard. Linearity is ensured by splitting environments on branching rules, requiring that the environment in the variable rule consists of just the variable, and the environment in the constant and unit rules are empty. Constants
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}
are typed using type schemas, which hold for any concrete assignment of types and priorities to their meta-variables. Instantiated type schemas are embedded into typing derivations using
\LabTirName
T-Const in
Fig.

2
,
e
.
g
., the type schema for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{send}}
can be instantiated with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}2}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}}
, and embedded using
\LabTirName
T-Const to give the following typing derivation:

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{send}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{{\mathbf{1}}\mathbin{\times}{!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}2}}{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{0}}}}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}2}}}{\mathbf{0}}}}

The typing rules treat all variables as linear resources, even those of non-linear types such as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}
, though they can easily be extended to allow values with unrestricted usage
[
Wad14
]
.

The only non-standard feature of the typing rules is the priority annotations. Priorities are based on obligations/capabilities used by Kobayashi
[
Kob06
]
, and simplified to single priorities following Padovani
[
Pad14
]
. The integration of priorities into GV is adapted from Padovani and Novara
[
PN15
]
. Paraphrasing Dardha and Gay
[
DG18b
]
, priorities obey the following two laws:

(i)

an action with lower priority happens before an action with higher priority; and

(ii)

communication requires
equal
priorities for dual actions.

In PGV, we keep track of a lower and upper bound on the priorities of a term,
i
.
e
., while evaluating the term, when it starts communicating, and when it finishes, respectively. The upper bound is written on the sequent and the lower bound is approximated from the typing environment,
e
.
g
., for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
the upper bound is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}
and the lower bound is at least
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})
. The latter is correct because a term cannot communicate at a priority earlier than the earliest priority amongst the channels it has access to. It is an
approximation
on function terms, as these can “skip” communication by returning the corresponding channel unused. However, linearity prevents such functions from being used in well typed configurations: once the unused channel’s priority has passed, it can no longer be used.

Typing rules for sequential constructs enforce sequentiality,
e
.
g
., the typing for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M;N}
has a side condition which requires that the upper bound of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
is smaller than the lower bound of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
,
i
.
e
.,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
finishes before
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
starts. The typing rule for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{new}}
ensures that both endpoints of a channel share the same priorities. Together, these two constraints guarantee deadlock freedom.

To illustrate this, let’s go back to the deadlocked program in
2.2
. Crucially, it composes the terms below in parallel. While each of these terms itself is well typed, they impose opposite conditions on the priorities, so connecting
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}x}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}x^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}y}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}y^{\prime}}
using
\LabTirName
T-Res is ill-typed, as there is no assignment to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o^{\prime}}
that can satsify both
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}<{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o^{\prime}}<{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
. (We omit the priorities on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}
.)

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o^{\prime}}}{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{?}}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o^{\prime}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{recv}\;{y^{\prime}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\mathbf{1}}\mathbin{\times}{\mathbf{end}_{?}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;{((),x)}\;\mathbf{in}\;\dots}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o^{\prime}}<{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o^{\prime}}}{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{?}}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;((),y^{\prime})\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;{y^{\prime}}\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;{((),x)}\;\mathbf{in}\;\dots}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{?}}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{recv}\;{x^{\prime}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\mathbf{1}}\mathbin{\times}{\mathbf{end}_{?}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o^{\prime}}}{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;y\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;{((),y)}\;\mathbf{in}\;\dots}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}<{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o^{\prime}}}{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{?}}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;((),x^{\prime})\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;{x^{\prime}}\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;y\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;{((),y)}\;\mathbf{in}\;\dots}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}

Closures suspend communication, so
\LabTirName
T-Lam stores the priority bounds of the function body on the function type, and
\LabTirName
T-App restores them. For instance,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda{x}.\mathbf{send}\;(x,y)}
is assigned the type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{S}}
,
i
.
e
., a function which, when applied, starts and finishes communicating at priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
.

\frac{\frac{\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{send}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{{T}\mathbin{\times}{!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{S}}}\frac{\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(x,y)}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\times}{!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{send}\;(x,y)}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{T}.{S}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda{x}.\mathbf{send}\;(x,y)}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{S}}}

For simplicity, we assume priority annotations are not inferred, but provided as an input to type checking. However, for any term, priorities can be inferred,
e
.
g
., by using the topological ordering of the directed graph where the vertices are the priority meta-variables and the edges are the inequality constraints between the priority meta-variables in the typing derivation.

Configurations Typing

Typing rules for configurations are at the bottom of
Fig.

2
. Configurations are typed by a judgement
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
stating that “a configuration
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
with flag
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}
is well typed under typing environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}
”. Configuration typing is based on the standard typing for GV. Terms are embedded either as main or as child threads. The priority bound from the term typing is discarded, as configurations contain no further blocking actions. Main threads are allowed to return a value, whereas child threads are required to return the unit value. Sequents are annotated with a flag
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}
, which ensures that there is at most one main thread.

While our configuration typing is based on the standard typing for GV, it differs on two counts:

(i)

we require that child threads return the unit value
, as opposed to a terminated channel; and

(ii)

we simplify typing for parallel composition
.

In order to guarantee deadlock freedom, in GV each parallel composition must split
exactly one
channel of the channel pseudo-type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\sharp}}
into two endpoints of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}
. Consequently, associativity of parallel composition does not preserve typing. In PGV, we guarantee deadlock freedom using priorities, which removes the need for the channel pseudo-type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\sharp}}
, and simplifies typing for parallel composition, while restoring type preservation for the structural congruence.

Typing Rules for Syntactic Sugar for PGV (

3
,

and

, cover syntactic sugar typing for PGV.

3.
Technical Developments

3.1.
Subject Reduction

Unlike with previous versions of GV, structural congruence, term reduction, and configuration reduction are all type preserving.

We must show that substitution preserves priority constraints. For this, we prove
3.1
, which shows that values have finished all their communication, and that any priorities in the type of the value come from the typing environment.

Lemma 3.1
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}
, and
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T})
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
.

Case

\LabTirName
T-Var.

Immediately.

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}

Case

\LabTirName
T-Const.

Immediately.

\frac{}{\varnothing\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}

Case

\LabTirName
T-Lam.

Immediately.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda x.M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{U}}}

Case

\LabTirName
T-Unit.

Immediately.

\frac{}{\varnothing\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}()}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}

Case

\LabTirName
T-Pair.

The induction hypotheses give us
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}
, hence
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}
, and
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T})
and
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U})
, hence
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})\sqcap\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T})\sqcap\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\times}{U}})
.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}W}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(V,W)}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\times}{U}}}

Case

\LabTirName
T-Inl.

The induction hypothesis gives us
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}
, and
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})=\pr{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T})}
. We know
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T})=\pr({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}})
, hence
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{U}})
.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\quad\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inl}\;V}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{U}}}

Case

\LabTirName
T-Inr.

The induction hypothesis gives us
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}
, and
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})=\pr{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U})}
. We know
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T})=\pr({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}})
, hence
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{U}})
.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}\quad\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inr}\;V}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{U}}}

Lemma 3.2
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Theta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Theta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M\{V/x\}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
.

Case

\LabTirName
T-Var.

By
3.1
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}
.

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}}}\xlongrightarrow{\scriptscriptstyle\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}/{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}\}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Theta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}}

\LabTirName

3.1
,
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Theta})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}})
, hence

.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda y.M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}})},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{U}}}\xlongrightarrow{\scriptscriptstyle\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}/{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}\}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Theta},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M\{V/x\}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Theta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda y.M\{V/x\}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Theta})},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{U}}}

Case

\LabTirName
T-App.

There are two subcases:

Subcase

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
.
Immediately, from the induction hypothesis.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q^{\prime}}}}{U}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta})\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}<{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q^{\prime}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M\;N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}\xlongrightarrow{\scriptscriptstyle\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}/{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}\}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Theta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M\{V/x\}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q^{\prime}}}}{U}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta})\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}<{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Theta}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}q^{\prime}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(M\{V/x\})\;N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}

Subcase

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
.
By
3.1
,
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Theta})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}})
, hence

.
Immediately, from the induction hypothesis.

Subcase

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
.
By
3.1
,
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Theta})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U^{\prime}})

and

.

We omit the cases where
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}

∎

Lemma 3.3
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}\longrightarrow_{M}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M^{\prime}}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
.

Proof.

The proof closely follows the standard proof of subject reduction for the simply-typed linear
\lambda
-calculus, as the constants are uninterpreted by the term reduction (
\longrightarrow_{M}
) and priority constraints are maintained consequence of
Section

3.1
.

By induction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}\longrightarrow_{M}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M^{\prime}}
.

\LabTirName

3.2
.

Lemma 3.4
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C^{\prime}}}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C^{\prime}}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of

∎

Theorem 3.5
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C^{\prime}}}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C^{\prime}}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C^{\prime}}}
.
