Subject Reduction (
\LabTirName
E-Send)

3.2.
Progress and Deadlock Freedom

PGV satisfies progress, as PGV configurations either reduce or are in normal form. However, the normal forms may seem surprising at first, as evaluating a well-typed PGV term does not necessarily produce just a value. If a term returns an endpoint, then its normal form contains a thread which is ready to communicate on the dual of that endpoint. This behaviour is not new to PGV.

Let us consider an example, adapted from Lindley and Morris
[
LM15
]
, in which a term returns an endpoint linked to an echo server. The echo server receives a value and sends it back unchanged. Consider the program which creates a new channel, with endpoints
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}
, spawns off an echo server listening on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
, and then returns
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(x,x^{\prime})\mathbin{=}\mathbf{new}\;()\;\mathbf{in}\;}\\
&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}\;(\lambda().\mathbf{echo}_{x});x^{\prime}}\end{array}\hfill\qquad\hfill\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{echo}_{x}}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(y,x)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;{x}\;\mathbf{in}\;}\\
&&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;(y,x)\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{close}\;x}\end{array}

If we reduce the above program, we get
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\circ\;\mathbf{echo}_{x}\parallel\bullet\;x^{\prime})}
. Clearly, no more evaluation is possible, even though the configuration contains the thread
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;\mathbf{echo}_{x}}
, which is blocked on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
. In
3.14
we will show that if a term does not return an endpoint, it must produce only a value.

Actions
are terms which perform communication actions and which synchronise between two threads.

Definition 3.6
.

A term
acts on
an endpoint
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
if it is
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{send}\;(V,x)}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{recv}\;{x}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{close}\;{x}}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{wait}\;{x}}
. A term is an
action
if it acts on some endpoint
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
.

Ready terms
are terms which perform communication actions, either by themselves,
e
.
g
., creating a new channel or thread, or with another thread,
e
.
g
., sending or receiving.
It is worth mentioning that the notion of readiness presented here is akin to live processes introduced by Caires and Pfenning
[
CP10
,
DP22
]
, and poised processes introduced by Pfenning and Griffith
[
PG15
]
and later used by Balzer
et al
.
[
BP17
,
BTP19
]
. Ready processes like live/poised processes denote processes that are ready to communicate on their providing channel.

Definition 3.7
.

A term
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}
is
ready
if it is of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E[M]}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
is of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{new}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}\;N}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}\;(x,y)}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
acts on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
. In the latter case, we say that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}
is
ready to act on

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
or is
blocked on
.

Progress
for the term language is standard for GV, and deviates from progress for linear
\lambda
-calculus only in that terms may reduce to values or
ready terms
, where the definition of ready terms encompasses all terms whose reduction is struck on some constant
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}
contains only session types, then:

(i)

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
is a value;

(ii)

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}\longrightarrow_{M}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
; or

(iii)

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
is ready.

With “
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}
contains only session types” we mean that for every
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
is a session type,
i
.
e
., is of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}
.

Canonical forms
deviate from those for GV, in that we opt to move all
\nu
-binders to the top. The standard GV canonical form, alternating
\nu
-binders and their corresponding parallel compositions, does not work for PGV, since multiple channels may be split across a single parallel composition.

A configuration either reduces, or it is equivalent to configuration in normal form. Crucial to the normal form is that each term
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{i}}
is blocked on the corresponding channel
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{i}}
, and hence no two terms act on dual endpoints. Furthermore, no term
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{i}}
can perform a communication action by itself, since those are excluded by the definition of actions.
Finally, as a corollary, we get that well-typed terms which do not return endpoints return just a value:

Definition 3.9
.

A configuration
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
is in canonical form if it is of the form

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x_{1}x^{\prime}_{1})\dots(\nu x_{n}x^{\prime}_{n})(\circ\;M_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel\circ\;M_{m}\parallel\bullet\;N)}

where no term
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{i}}
is a value.

Lemma 3.10
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
, there exists some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D}}
is in canonical form.

Proof.

We move any
\nu
-binders to the top using
\LabTirName
SC-ResExt, discard any superfluous occurrences of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;()}
using
\LabTirName
SC-ParNil, and move the main thread to the rightmost position using
\LabTirName
SC-ParComm and
\LabTirName
SC-ParAssoc.
∎

Definition 3.11
.

A configuration
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
is in normal form if it is of the form

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x_{1}x^{\prime}_{1})\dots(\nu x_{n}x^{\prime}_{n})(\circ\;M_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel\circ\;M_{m}\parallel\bullet\;V)}

where each
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{i}}
is ready to act on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{i}}
.

Lemma 3.12
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
is ready to act on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}
, then the priority bound
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}
is some priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
, i.e., not
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}}
.

Proof.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E[M]}
. By induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E}
.
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
has priority
\pr({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}})
, and each constructor of the evaluation context
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E}
passes on the
maximum
of the priorities of its premises. No rule introduces the priority bound
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}}
on the sequent.
∎

Theorem 3.13
.

If
\varnothing\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
is in canonical form, then either
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D}}
; or
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}\equiv\conf{D}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{D}}
in normal form.

Proof.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x_{1}x^{\prime}_{1})\dots(\nu x_{n}x^{\prime}_{n})(\circ\;M_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel\circ\;M_{m}\parallel\bullet\;N)}
.
We apply
3.8
to each
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
. If for any
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{i}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
we obtain a reduction
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{i}}\longrightarrow_{M}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M^{\prime}_{i}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}\longrightarrow_{M}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N^{\prime}}
, we apply
\LabTirName
E-LiftM and
\LabTirName
E-LiftC to obtain a reduction on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
.
Otherwise, each term
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{i}}
is ready, and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
is either ready or a value.
Pick the
ready
term
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{1}},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{m}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}\}
with the smallest priority bound.

(1)

If
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}
is a new
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E[\mathbf{new}\;()]}
, we apply
\LabTirName
E-New.

(2)

If
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}
is a spawn
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E[\mathbf{spawn}\;M]}
, we apply
\LabTirName
E-Spawn.

(3)

If
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}
is a link
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E[\mathbf{link}\;(y,z)]}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E[\mathbf{link}\;(z,y)]}
, we apply
\LabTirName
E-Link.

(4)

Otherwise,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}
is ready to act on some endpoint
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}
. Let
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}
be the dual endpoint of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
. The typing rules enforce the linear use of endpoints, so there must be a term
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L^{\prime}}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{1}},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{m}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}\}
which uses
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
.
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L^{\prime}}
must be either a ready term or a value:

(a)

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L^{\prime}}
is ready. By
3.12
, the priority of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}
is
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S})
. By duality,
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S})
.
We cannot have
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L^{\prime}}
, otherwise the action on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
would be guarded by the action on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
, requiring
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}})<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S})
.

The term
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L^{\prime}}
must be ready to act on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
, otherwise the action
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
would be guarded by another action with priority smaller than
\pr{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S})}
, which contradicts our choice of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}
as having the smallest priority.

Therefore, we have two terms ready to act on dual endpoints. We apply the appropriate reduction rule,
i
.
e
.,
\LabTirName
E-Send or
\LabTirName
E-Close.

(b)

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
and is a value. We rewrite
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
to put
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}
in the position corresponding to the endpoint it is blocked on, using
\LabTirName
SC-ParComm,
\LabTirName
SC-ParAssoc, and optionally
\LabTirName
SC-ResSwap.
We then repeat the steps above with the term with the next smallest priority, until either we find a reduction, or the configuration has reached the desired normal form.

The argument based on the priority being the smallest continues to hold, since we know that neither
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}
nor
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L^{\prime}}
will be picked, and no other term uses
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
.

∎

Corollary 3.14
.

If
\varnothing\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}_{\!\!\conf{C}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}
contains no endpoints, then
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi\;V}
for some value
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}
.

An immediate consequence of
3.13
and
3.14
is that
a term which does not return an endpoint will complete all its communication actions, thus satisfying deadlock freedom.

4.
Relation to Priority CP

Thus far we have presented Priority GV (PGV) together with the relevant technical results. We remind the reader that this line of work of adding priorities, started with Priority CP (PCP)
[
DG18a
]
where priorities are integrated in Wadler’s Classical Processes (CP), which is a
\pi
-calculus leveraging the correspondence of session types as linear logic propositions
[
Wad12
]
.
In his work, Wadler presents a connection (via encoding) of CP and GV. Following that work, we sat out to understand the connection between the priority versions of CP and GV, thus comparing PGV and PCP.
Before presenting our formal results, we will revisit PCP in the following section.

4.1.
Revisiting Priority CP

Types

Types (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}}
) in PCP are based on classical linear logic propositions, and are defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\otimes^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\invamp^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bot^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\with^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\top^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\end{array}

Each connective is annotated with a priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}\in\mathbb{N}
.

Types
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\otimes^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\invamp^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}}
type the endpoints of a channel over which we send or receive a channel of type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}
, and then proceed as type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}}
. Types
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\bot}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}]}}
type the endpoints of a channel whose session has terminated, and over which we send or receive a
ping
before closing the channel. These two types act as units for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\otimes^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\invamp^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}}
, respectively.

Types
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\with^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}}
type the endpoints of a channel over which we can receive or send a choice between two branches
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}
or
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}}
. We have opted for a simplified version of choice and followed the original Wadler’s CP
[
Wad14
]
, however types
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\oplus}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\with}
can be trivially generalised to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}\{l_{i}:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\with^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}\{l_{i}:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}}}
, respectively, as in the original PCP
[
DG18b
]
.

Types
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\top^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}}
type the endpoints of a channel over which we can send or receive a choice between no options. These two types act as units for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\with^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}}
, respectively.

Typing Environments

Typing environments
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}}
associate names to types. Environments are linear, so two environments can only be combined as
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}}
if their names are distinct,
i
.
e
.,
{\fv({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})\cap\fv({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta})=\varnothing}
.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\varnothing}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}\end{array}

Type Duality

Duality is an involutive function on types which preserves priorities:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}(\mathbf{1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bot^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}(\bot^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}({A}\mathbin{\otimes^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A^{\bot}}\mathbin{\invamp^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B^{\bot}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}({A}\mathbin{\invamp^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A^{\bot}}\mathbin{\otimes^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B^{\bot}}}\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}(\mathbf{0}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\top^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}(\top^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}({A}\mathbin{\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A^{\bot}}\mathbin{\with^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B^{\bot}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}({A}\mathbin{\with^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A^{\bot}}\mathbin{\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B^{\bot}}}\end{array}

Priorities

The function
\pr(\cdot)
returns smallest priority of a type. As with PGV, the type system guarantees that the top-most connective always holds the smallest priority. The function
\pr(\cdot)
returns the
minimum
priority of all types a typing context, or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}}
if the context is empty:

\begin{array}[]{lclclcl}\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}})&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}\\
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bot^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}})&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{lclclcl}\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\otimes^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}})&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}\\
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\invamp^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}})&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{lclclcl}\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}})&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}\\
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\top^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}})&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{lclclcl}\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}})&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}\\
\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\with^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}})&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}\end{array}

\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\varnothing})={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\top\!}}\quad\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T})=\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})\sqcap\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T})

Terms

Processes (
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}
) in PCP are defined by the following grammar.

\begin{array}[t]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(P\parallel Q)}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}\\
&\;\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].P}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[].P}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x(y).P}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x().P}\\
&\;\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inl}.{P}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inr}.{P}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:P;\text{inr}:Q\}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\{\}}\end{array}

Process
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}}
links endpoints
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}}
and forwards communication from one to the other.
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(P\parallel Q)}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}}
denote respectively the restriction processes where channel endpoints
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}}
are bound together and with scope
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
, the parallel composition of processes
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}
and the terminated process.

Processes
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].P}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x(y).P}}
send or receive over channel
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
a value
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}}
and proceed as process
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
. Processes
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[].P}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x().P}}
send and receive an empty value—denoting the closure of channel
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
, and continue as
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
.

Processes
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inl}.{P}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inr}.{P}}}
make a left and right choice, respectively and proceed as process
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
. Dually,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:P;\text{inr}:Q\}}}
offers both left and right branches, with continuations
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}
, and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\{\}}}
is the empty offer.

We write
unbound
send as
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x\langle{y}\rangle.P}}
, which is syntactic sugar for
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[z].({y}{\leftrightarrow}{z}\parallel P)}}
. Alternatively, we could take
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x\langle{y}\rangle.P}}
as primitive, and let
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].P}}
be syntactic sugar for
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{y}{z}){(x\langle{z}\rangle.P)}}}
. CP takes
bound
sending as primitive, as it is impossible to eliminate the top-level cut in terms such as
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{y}{z}){(x\langle{z}\rangle.P)}}}
, even with commuting conversions. In our setting without commuting conversions and with more permissive normal forms, this is no longer an issue, but, for simplicity, we keep bound sending as primitive.

On Commuting Conversions

The main change we make to PCP is removing commuting conversions. Commuting conversions are necessary if we want our reduction strategy to correspond exactly to cut (or cycle in
[
DG18b
]
) elimination. However, as Lindley and Morris
[
LM15
]
show, all communications that can be performed
with
the use of commuting conversions, can also be performed
without
them, but using structural congruence.

From the perspective of process calculi, commuting conversions behave strangely.
Consider the commuting conversion
(\kappa_{\invamp})
for
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x(y).P}}
:

(\kappa_{\invamp})\quad{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{z}{z^{\prime}}){(x(y).P\parallel Q)}}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x(y).(\nu{z}{z^{\prime}}){(P\parallel Q)}}

As a result of
(\kappa_{\invamp})
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}
becomes blocked on
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x(y)}}
, and any actions
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}
was able to perform become unavailable. Consequently, CP is non-confluent:

\begin{array}[]{cc}\lx@intercol\hfil\hskip 43.05542pt{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){(a(y).P\parallel(\nu{z}{z^{\prime}}){(z[].\mathbf{0}\parallel z^{\prime}().Q)})}}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\qquad\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\Longrightarrow\hphantom{{}^{+}}$}&\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\Longrightarrow^{+}$}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}a(y).(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){(P\parallel(\nu{z}{z^{\prime}}){(z[].\mathbf{0}\parallel z^{\prime}().Q)})}}}&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}a(y).(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){(P\parallel Q)}}}\end{array}

In PCP, commuting conversions break our intuition that an action with lower priority occurs before an action with higher priority. To cite Dardha and Gay
[
DG18b
]
“
if a prefix on a channel endpoint
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
with priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
is pulled out at top level, then to preserve priority constraints in the typing rules [..], it is necessary to increase priorities of all actions after the prefix on
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
” by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o+1}
.

4.2.
Operational Semantics

The operational semantics for PCP, given in
Fig.

7
, is defined as a reduction relation
{\Longrightarrow}
on processes (bottom) and uses structural congruence (top). Each of the axioms of structural congruence corresponds to the axiom of the same name for PGV. We write
{\Longrightarrow^{+}}
for the transitive closures, and
{\Longrightarrow^{\star}}
for the reflexive-transitive closures.

The reduction relation is given by a set of axioms and inference rules for context closure. Reduction occurs under restriction.
\LabTirName{E-Link}
reduces a parallel composition with a link into a substitution.
\LabTirName{E-Send}
is the main communication rule, where send and receive processes sychronise and reduce to the corresponding continuations.
\LabTirName{E-Close}
follows the previous rule and it closes the channel identified by endpoints
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}}
.
\LabTirName{E-Select-Inl}
and
\LabTirName{E-Select-Inr}
are generalised versions of
\LabTirName{E-Send}
. They state respectively that a left and right selection synchronises with a choice offering and reduces to the corresponding continuations. The last three rules state that reduction is closed under restriction, parallel composition and structural congruence, respectively.

Structural congruence.

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\LabTirName{SC-LinkSwap}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{y}{\leftrightarrow}{x}}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ResLink}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ResSwap}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{y}{x}){P}}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ResComm}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){(\nu{z}{w}){P}}}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{z}{w}){(\nu{x}{y}){P}}}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ResExt}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){(P\parallel Q)}}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel(\nu{x}{y}){Q}},\text{ if }{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}\notin\fv({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P})}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ParNil}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel\mathbf{0}}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ParComm}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q\parallel P}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ParAssoc}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel(Q\parallel R)}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(P\parallel Q)\parallel R}\end{array}

Reduction.

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\LabTirName{E-Link}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){({w}{\leftrightarrow}{x}\parallel P)}}&\Longrightarrow&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\{w/x\}}\\
\LabTirName{E-Send}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){(x[z].P\parallel x(w).Q)}}&\Longrightarrow&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){(\nu{z}{w}){(P\parallel Q)}}}\\
\LabTirName{E-Close}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){(x[].P\parallel y().Q)}}&\Longrightarrow&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q}\\
\LabTirName{E-Select-Inl}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){({x}\triangleleft\text{inl}.{P}\parallel{x}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:Q;\text{inr}:R\})}}&\Longrightarrow&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){(P\parallel Q)}}\\
\LabTirName{E-Select-Inr}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){({x}\triangleleft\text{inr}.{P}\parallel{x}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:Q;\text{inr}:R\})}}&\Longrightarrow&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){(P\parallel R)}}\end{array}\\
\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P^{\prime}}}}\quad\textsc{lab=E-LiftRes}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}\parallel Q}}\quad\textsc{lab=E-LiftPar}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q^{\prime}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q^{\prime}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}\quad\textsc{lab=E-LiftSC}

Figure 7.
Operational Semantic for PCP.

4.3.
Typing Rules

Figure

8
gives the typing rules for our version of PCP. A typing judgement
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{\Gamma}}
states that “process
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
is well typed under the typing context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}
”.

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}{y}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Link}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Res}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Par}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}\vdash{\varnothing}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Halt}\\
\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B})}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\otimes^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Send}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[].P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Close}\\
\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B})}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x(y).P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\invamp^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Recv}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x().P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bot^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Wait}\\

[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\top^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Offer-Absurd}

Figure 8.
Typing Rules for PCP.

T-Link
states that the link process
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}}
is well typed under channels
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}}
having dual types, respectively
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}}}
.
T-Res
states that the restriction process
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}}
is well typed under typing context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}
if process
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
is well typed in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}
augmented with channel endpoints
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}}
having dual types, respectively
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}}}
.
T-Par
states that the parallel composition of processes
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}
is well typed under the disjoint union of their respective typing contexts.
T-Halt
states that the terminated process
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}}
is well typed in the empty context.

T-Send
and
T-Recv
state that the sending and receiving of a bound name
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}}
over a channel
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
is well typed under
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
of type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\otimes^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}}
, respectively
{{A}\mathbin{\invamp^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}
. Priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
is the smallest among all priorities of the types used by the output or input process, captured by the side condition
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B})}
.

Rules
T-Close
and
T-Wait
type the closure of channel
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
and are in the same lines as the previous two rules, requiring that the priority of channel
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
is the smallest among all priorities in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}
.

T-Select-Inl
and
T-Select-Inr
type respectively the left
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inl}.{P}}}
and right
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inr}.{P}}}
choice performed on channel
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
.
T-Offer
and
T-Offer-Absurd
type the offering of a choice, or empty choice, on channel
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
. In all the above rules the priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
of channel
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}
is the smallest with respect to the typing context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma})}
and types involved in the choice
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}<\pr({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B})}
.

Figure

9
shows how syntactic sugar in PCP is well typed.

\frac{\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}<\pr{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B})}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x\langle{y}\rangle.P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\otimes}{B}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-UnboundSend}\triangleq\frac{\frac{\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{z}{\leftrightarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}{y}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{z}{\leftrightarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}{y}\parallel P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}<\pr{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B})}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[z].({z}{\leftrightarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}{y}\parallel P)}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\otimes}{B}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}}}}

Figure 9.
Typing Rules for Syntactic Sugar for PCP.

Finally, since our reduction relation is a strict subset of the reduction relation in the original
[
DG18b
]
, we defer to their proof of subject reduction (Theorem 2 in
[
DG18b
]
). We prove progress for our version of PCP, see
Section

4.5
.

4.4.
PCP and PLL

In this subsection, we highlight the connection between PCP and linear logic.
Dardha and Gay
[
DG18a
]
present PCP–consequently also PCP given in this paper–in a way which can be viewed both as Classical Processes with restriction (T-Res) and parallel composition (T-Par) typing rules, and as a new version of linear logic, which they call Priority Linear Logic (PLL).
PLL builds on CLL by replacing the cut rule with two logical rules: a mix and a cycle rule—here corresponding to T-Par and T-Res, respectively. Dardha and Gay
[
DG18a
, §4]
prove cycle-elimination, in the same lines as cut-elimination for CLL. As a corollary of cycle-elimination for PLL, we obtain deadlock freedom for PCP (Theorem 3 in
[
DG18a
, §4]
). In summary, PLL is an extension of CLL and the authors show the correspondence of PCP and PLL. Notice however, that PCP is not in correspondence with CLL itself, since processes in PCP are graphs, whether CLL induces trees.

4.5.
Technical Developments

Definition 4.1
.

A process acts on an endpoint
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
if it is
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{y}{\leftrightarrow}{x}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].P}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x(y).P}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[].P}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x().P}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inl}.{P}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inr}.{P}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:P;\text{inr}:Q\}}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\{\}}
. A process is an action if it acts on some endpoint
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
.

Definition 4.2
.

A process
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}
is in canonical form if it is either
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}
or of the form

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x_{1}}{x^{\prime}_{1}}){\dots(\nu{x_{n}}{x^{\prime}_{n}}){(P_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel P_{m})}}}
where
m>0
and each
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{j}}
is an action.

Lemma 4.3
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}
, there exists some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
is in canonical form.

Proof.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}
, we are done. Otherwise, we move any
\nu
-binders to the top using
\LabTirName
SC-ResExt, and discard any superfluous occurrences of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}
using
\LabTirName
SC-ParNil.
∎

The proof for progress (below) follows the same reasoning by Kobayashi
[
Kob06
]
used in the proof of deadlock freedom for closed processes (Theorem 2).

Theorem 4.4
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{\varnothing}}
, then either
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}}
or there exists a
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}
.

Proof (Sketch).

This proof follows the exact same reasoning and proof sketch given by Kobayashi in
[
Kob06
]
and later adopted by Dardha and Gay for PCP in their technical report
[
DG18b
]
.

By
4.3
, we rewrite
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}
to canonical form. If the resulting process is
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}
, we are done. Otherwise, it is of the form

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x_{1}}{x^{\prime}_{1}}){\dots(\nu{x_{n}}{x^{\prime}_{n}}){(P_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel P_{m})}}}\vdash{\varnothing}

where
m>0
and each
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{i}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{i}}}
is an action.

Consider processes
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel P_{m}}
. Among them, we pick the process with the smallest priority
\pr{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{i}})}
for all
i
. Let this process be
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{i}}
and the priority of the top prefix be
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
.
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{i}}
acts on some endpoint
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{i}}
. We must have
\pr{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{i}})}=\pr{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A})}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
, since the other actions in
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{i}}
are guarded by the action on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}
, thus satisfying law (i) of priorities.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{i}}
is a link
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{y}{\leftrightarrow}{z}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{z}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}
, we apply
\LabTirName
E-Link.

Otherwise,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{i}}
is an input/branching or output/selection action on endpoint
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}
with priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
. Since process
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}
is closed and consequently it respects law (ii) of priorities, there must be a co-action
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
are dual endpoints of the same channel (by application of rule
T-Res
). By duality,
\pr{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A})}=\pr{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}})}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
. In the following we show that:
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
is the subject of a top level action of a process
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{j}}
with
i\neq j
. This allows for the communication among
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{j}}
to happen immediately over channel endpoints
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
.

Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
is an action not in a different parallel process
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{j}}
but rather of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{i}}
itself. That means that the action on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
must be prefixed by the action on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
, which is top level in
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{i}}
. To respect law (i) of priorities we must have
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}<{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
, which is absurd. This means that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
is in another parallel process
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{j}}
for
i\neq j
.

Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P_{j}}
is not at top level. In order to respect law (i) of priorities, it means that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
is prefixed by actions that are smaller than its priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
. This leads to a contradiction because stated that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}
is the smallest priority. Hence,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}
must be the subject of a top level action.

We have two processes, acting on dual endpoints. We apply the appropriate reduction rule,
i
.
e
.,
\LabTirName
E-Send,
\LabTirName
E-Close,
\LabTirName
E-Select-Inl, or
\LabTirName
E-Select-Inr.
∎

4.6.
Correspondence between PGV and PCP

We illustrate the relation between PCP and PGV by defining a translation
\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}
from PCP processes to PGV configurations which satisfies operational correspondence.

The translation
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}
translates PCP processes to PGV configurations by translating as much as possible of the
\pi
-calculus constructs from PCP to the identical configuration constructs in PGV,
i
.
e
., all top-level
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\nu}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\parallel}
constructs. When it encounters the first action, it translates the remainder of the processs to a term using
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}
.
The translation
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}
translates PCP processes to PGV terms by mapping the
\pi
-calculus constructs from PCP (
e
.
g
.,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\nu}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\parallel}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\cdot[\cdot].\cdot}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\cdot[\cdot].\cdot}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\cdot(\cdot).\cdot}}
,
etc
.) to the corresponding constants in PGV (
e
.
g
.,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{new}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{send}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{recv}}}
,
etc
.).
Translating a process with
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}
is the same as translating that process with
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}
followed by several steps of configuration reduction.
The translation
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis}
tranlates session types from PCP to session types in PGV matching the translation on processes.

The translation on types is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llparenthesis{{A}\mathbin{\otimes^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}\rrparenthesis}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{\overline{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}}.{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llparenthesis{{A}\mathbin{\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}\rrparenthesis}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\mathbin{\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}}}\end{array}\hfill\quad\hfill\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llparenthesis{\mathbf{1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\rrparenthesis}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\mathbf{end}_{!}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llparenthesis{\mathbf{0}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\rrparenthesis}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\oplus}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}\{\}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llparenthesis{{A}\mathbin{\invamp^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}\rrparenthesis}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}.{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llparenthesis{{A}\mathbin{\with^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{B}}\rrparenthesis}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\mathbin{\with^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}}}\end{array}\hfill\quad\hfill\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llparenthesis{\bot^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\rrparenthesis}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\mathbf{end}_{?}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llparenthesis{\top^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}}\rrparenthesis}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\with}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}o}}\{\}}}\end{array}

The translation
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}
translates processes to
terms
and maps the
\pi
-calculus constructs from PCP to the corresponding constants in PGV:

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}\;{(x,y)}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis(\nu{x}{y}){P}\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{new}\;()\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\parallel Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}\;{(\lambda().\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}})};\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis\mathbf{0}\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}()}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis x[].P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{close}\;{x};\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis x().P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{wait}\;{x};\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis x[y].P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(y,z)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{new}\;()\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;{(z,x)}\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis x(y).P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(y,x)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;{x}\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{x}\triangleleft\text{inl}.{P}\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{select}\;\mathbf{inl}\;{x}\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{x}\triangleleft\text{inr}.{P}\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{select}\;\mathbf{inr}\;{x}\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{x}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:P;\text{inr}:Q\}\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{offer}\;x\;\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}};\mathbf{inr}\;x\mapsto{\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}\}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{x}\triangleright\{\}\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{offer}\;x\;\{\}}}

Unfortunately, the operational correspondence along
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}
is unsound, as it translates
\nu
-binders and parallel compositions to
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{new}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}}}
, which can reduce to their equivalent configuration constructs using
\LabTirName
E-New and
\LabTirName
E-Spawn. The same goes for
\nu
-binders which are inserted when translating bound send to unbound send. For instance, the process
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].P}}
is blocked, but its translation uses
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{new}}}
and can reduce.
To address this issue, we introduce a second translation,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}
, which is equivalent to translating with
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}
then reducing with
\LabTirName
E-New and
\LabTirName
E-Spawn:

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis(\nu{x}{y}){P}\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\parallel Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}\parallel\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis x[y].P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu yz)(\circ\;\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;(z,x)\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}})}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{x}\triangleleft\text{inl}.{P}\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu yz)(\circ\;\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{close}\;(\mathbf{send}\;(\mathbf{inl}\;y,x));z\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}})}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{x}\triangleleft\text{inr}.{P}\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu yz)(\circ\;\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{close}\;(\mathbf{send}\;(\mathbf{inr}\;y,x));z\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}})}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ{\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}},\quad\text{if none of the above apply}

Typing environments are translated pointwise, and sequents
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}}
are translated as
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\rrparenthesis}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\circ}}
indicates a child thread, since translated processes do not have a main thread.
The translations
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}
preserve typing, and the latter induces a sound and complete operational correspondence.

Lemma 4.5
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}}
, then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llparenthesis\Gamma\rrparenthesis}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}p}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}}
.

Case

\LabTirName
T-Link,
\LabTirName
T-Res,
\LabTirName
T-Par, and
\LabTirName
T-Halt.

See
Fig.

10
.

Case

\LabTirName
T-Close, and
\LabTirName
T-Wait.

See
Fig.

11
.

Case

\LabTirName
T-Send.

See
Fig.

12
.

Case

\LabTirName
T-Recv.

See
Fig.

13
.

Case

\LabTirName
T-Select-Inl,
\LabTirName
T-Select-Inr, and
\LabTirName
T-Offer.

See
Fig.

14
.

∎

{\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}{y}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}}}}\quad\textsc{lab=T-Link}}\xlongrightarrow{\;\raisebox{2.0pt}{${\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}$}\;}{\frac{\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\mathbin{\times}{\overline{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}}}\mathbin{\multimap}{\mathbf{1}}}}\quad\frac{\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5703,0.5}\bot\!}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llparenthesis Theorem 4.6
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}}
, then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llparenthesis\Gamma\rrparenthesis}\vdash^{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}}
.

Case

\LabTirName

Immediately, from the induction hypothesis.

Theorem 4.7
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}}
, there exists a
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\Longrightarrow^{+}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{\star}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}}
.
We omit the cases which cannot occur as their left-hand side term forms are not in the image of the translation function,
i
.
e
.,
\LabTirName
E-New,
\LabTirName
E-Spawn, and
\LabTirName
E-LiftM.

Case

\LabTirName
E-Link.

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\conf{F}[\mathbf{link}\;(w,x)]\parallel\conf{C})}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}[()]\parallel\conf{C}\{w/x^{\prime}\}}}

The source for
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}\;(w,x)}}

must
be
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{w}{\leftrightarrow}{x}}}
. None of the translation rules introduce an evaluation context around the recursive call, hence
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}}}
must be the empty context. Let
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
be the source term for
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}}
, i.e.,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}}
. Hence, we have:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){({w}{\leftrightarrow}{x}\parallel P)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\{w/x^{\prime}\}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\circ\;\mathbf{link}\;(w,x)\parallel\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}\{w/x^{\prime}\}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\{w/x^{\prime}\}\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Longrightarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}

Case

\LabTirName
E-Send.

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\conf{F}[\mathbf{send}\;{(V,x)}]\parallel\conf{F^{\prime}}[\mathbf{recv}\;{x^{\prime}}])}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\conf{F}[x]\parallel\conf{F^{\prime}}[(V,x^{\prime})])}}

There are three possible sources for
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{send}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{recv}}}
:
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].P}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}(y^{\prime}).Q}}
;
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inl}.{P}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x^{\prime}}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:Q;\text{inr}:R\}}}
; or
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inr}.{P}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x^{\prime}}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:Q;\text{inr}:R\}}}
.

Subcase

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].P}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}(y^{\prime}).Q}}
.
None of the translation rules introduce an evaluation context around the recursive call, hence
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}}}
must be
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\square\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}
. Similarly,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F^{\prime}}}}
must be
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;\mathbf{let}\;(y^{\prime},x^{\prime})\mathbin{=}\square\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}
. The value
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}}
must be an endpoint
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
, bound by the name restriction
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu yy^{\prime})}}
introduced by the translation. Hence, we have:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){(x[y].P\parallel x^{\prime}(y^{\prime}).Q)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){(\nu{y}{y^{\prime}}){(P\parallel Q)}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\nu yy^{\prime})\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\circ\;\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;{(y,x)}\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}\parallel\\
\circ\;\mathbf{let}\;(y^{\prime},x^{\prime})\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;{x^{\prime}}\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}\end{array}\right)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\nu yy^{\prime})(\circ\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}\parallel\circ\;\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\nu yy^{\prime})(\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}\parallel\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Longrightarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\equiv\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{\star}}}

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\conf{F}[\mathbf{wait}\;{x}]\parallel\conf{F^{\prime}}[\mathbf{close}\;{x^{\prime}}])}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{F}[()]\parallel\conf{F^{\prime}}[()]}}

Lemma 4.8
.

For any
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
, either:

•

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}
; or

•

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{+}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}
, and for any
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}}
, if
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}}
, then
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\conf{C}}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{\star}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
.

Case

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}}
.

We have:

\begin{array}[]{lrll}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis(\nu{x}{y}){P}\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{new}\;()\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}\\
&{\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{+}}&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\circ\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}})}}\\
&{\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{\star}}&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}\\
&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis(\nu{x}{y}){P}\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}\end{array}

Case

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q}}
.

\begin{array}[]{lrll}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\parallel Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;\mathbf{spawn}\;(\lambda().\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}});\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}\\
&{\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{+}}&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}\parallel\circ\;\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}\\
&{\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{\star}}&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}\parallel\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}\\
&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\parallel Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}\end{array}

Case

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].P}}
.

\begin{array}[]{lrll}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis x[y].P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(y,z)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{new}\;()\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;{(z,x)}\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}\\
&{\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{+}}&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu yz)(\circ\;\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{send}\;(z,x)\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}})}}\\
&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis x[y].P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}\end{array}

Case

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inl}.{P}}}
.

\begin{array}[]{lrll}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis x[y].P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{select}\;\mathbf{inl}\;{x}\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}\\
&\triangleq&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{let}\;(y,z)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{new}\;()\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{close}\;(\mathbf{send}\;{(\mathbf{inl}\;y,x)});z\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}\\
&{\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{+}}&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu yz)(\circ\;\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{close}\;(\mathbf{send}\;(\mathbf{inl}\;y,x));z\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}})}}\\
&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis x[y].P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}\end{array}

Case

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inr}.{P}}}
.

\begin{array}[]{lrll}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis x[y].P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{select}\;\mathbf{inr}\;{x}\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}\\
&\triangleq&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{let}\;(y,z)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{new}\;()\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{close}\;(\mathbf{send}\;{(\mathbf{inr}\;y,x)});z\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}}\\
&{\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{+}}&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu yz)(\circ\;\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{close}\;(\mathbf{send}\;(\mathbf{inr}\;y,x));z\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}})}}\\
&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis x[y].P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}\end{array}

Case

*
.

In all other cases,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}
,
then
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}
.

Proof.

Every axiom of the structural congruence in PCP maps directly to the axiom of the same name in PGV.
∎

Theorem 4.10
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}
,
then
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{+}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}
.

By induction on the derivation of
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}\parallel\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Longrightarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\conf{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\longrightarrow_{\conf{C}}^{\star}}}
(by
4.8
)

Case

\LabTirName

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){({x}\triangleleft\text{inl}.{P}\parallel{x}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:Q;\text{inr}:R\})}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){(P\parallel Q)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\circ\;\mathbf{let}\;x\mathbin{=}\mathbf{select}\;\mathbf{inl}\;{x}\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}\parallel\\
\circ\;\mathbf{offer}\;x^{\prime}\;\{\mathbf{inl}\;x^{\prime}\mapsto{\llparenthesis Q\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}};\mathbf{inr}\;x^{\prime}\mapsto{\llparenthesis R\rrparenthesis_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{M}}}\}\end{array}\right)}}}
